can you take a look at this?
Yes — I took a look at the app code you pasted, and it’s a solid prototype, but there are a few concrete issues that are likely to cause real problems.

The biggest ones:

Event loop shutdown is risky in main.py

main() starts a QEventLoop and runs it with loop.run_forever().
Then closeEvent() tries to do loop.run_until_complete(asyncio.sleep(...)) while the loop is already active.
That can deadlock, stall shutdown, or behave unpredictably depending on the Qt/async timing.
Better pattern: do disconnect logic with asyncio.create_task(...) or schedule a one-off async shutdown task instead of re-entering the currently running loop.
MusicEngine.start_system_loopback() can crash on machines without a valid default output

It does:
out_dev = output_index if output_index is not None else sd.default.device[1]
then sd.query_devices(out_dev)
On some Windows setups, sd.default.device can be missing, empty, or not a valid output index. Then you get IndexError, TypeError, or a bad device selection before the fallback logic ever runs.
This needs a guard: validate sd.default.device and ensure the chosen index exists before using it.
Audio analysis is too lossy

In MusicEngine.analyse(), it drains the queue and keeps only the last chunk:
latest = self.audio_q.get_nowait() in a loop, then uses only the final item
That means you’re throwing away most of the audio data and only analyzing the last block. For music-reactive effects, this can make patterns stutter or become laggy.
Better: accumulate a rolling buffer or average recent frames rather than keeping just one chunk.
load_audio() is “sticky-fail”

It sets AUDIO_TRIED = True and then returns AUDIO_OK.
If sounddevice or numpy import fails once, the app never tries again.
That is fine if the environment is fixed, but it can get annoying during packaging or on systems where audio libraries become available later.
If you want resilience, either allow retries or make the error message clearer.
start_system_loopback() fallback logic is okay but not very robust

The code tries WASAPI loopback first, then looks for "loopback" or "stereo mix" in device names.
That’s reasonable, but on some setups, the device names differ or the output device is a hidden/unsupported one, so it may fail without a good user-facing explanation.
closeEvent() also disconnects devices in a blocking loop

It creates a coroutine and then manually spins the event loop for up to 3 seconds.
That is another shutdown pattern that can freeze or misbehave under GUI event handling.
The code is overall fairly clean and readable, but the above are the main issues I’d fix first.

If you want, I can do one of these next:

give you a “critical bug list” sorted by severity,
propose exact code fixes for the shutdown/audio problems,
or help trace what this app is trying to do and whether this is a BLE protocol issue vs. a UI/audio issue.

@echo off
setlocal

REM --- Self-elevate to admin if not already ---
net session >nul 2>&1
if %errorlevel% neq 0 (
    echo Requesting administrator privileges...
    powershell -NoProfile -Command "Start-Process -FilePath '%~f0' -Verb RunAs"
    exit /b
)

REM --- Now running elevated ---
cd /d "%~dp0"

where pythonw >nul 2>nul
if %errorlevel%==0 (
    start "" pythonw "%~dp0main.py"
    exit /b 0
)

where python >nul 2>nul
if %errorlevel%==0 (
    start "" python "%~dp0main.py"
    exit /b 0
)

echo Python is not installed or not on PATH.
echo Install Python from python.org and try again.
pause

I’m synced up with the current state from the files you pasted. The most important discovery is that the white-channel problem is almost certainly caused by this line in mrstar_color():

Python
w = 0xFFFF if pure else 0x0000
For the MR Star protocol, that appears to enable the controller’s white channel whenever “Full saturation” is checked. That explains:

Red containing white
Green and blue appearing pale
Yellow becoming yellow-green/washed out
Pink and music modes being dominated by white
Brightness behaving incorrectly
Static colors looking wrong
The likely correction is:

Python
w = 0x0000 if pure else 0xFFFF
However, I do not want to blindly reverse it without testing the protocol against your actual strips. The meaning of the WW WW field needs to be verified against the MR Star command format and your controller behavior.

There are also several separate issues visible in the current code:

The app does not read the actual LED state

dev.last_color only stores the last color sent by the application.
The layout tab is therefore not showing the strip’s real state.
BLE write success only means the Bluetooth characteristic accepted the write. It is not confirmation that the LED controller applied the command.
If the MR Star controller exposes no readable state or notification characteristic, a camera/color sensor is required for true visual feedback.
The “Scroll Music” macro is not actually starting the selected music source

It only sends the scroll commands:
Python
SCROLL_MACRO = SCROLL_SETUP + SCROLL_DATA + SCROLL_END
After the macro finishes, nothing in _play_macro() starts microphone, AUX, loopback, or file analysis.
The apparent switch to the computer microphone is probably the controller’s own behavior or a separate application state, not this Python macro.
The music source names are not yet correct

The current app exposes:
Microphone
System audio (loopback)
Music file
A real AUX input may appear in Windows as:
Line In
Aux
External In
Stereo Mix
What U Hear
a USB audio device name
The application should distinguish input capture devices from output loopback devices instead of calling every input “Microphone.”
The music effects currently send too many commands

_music_tick() runs every 80 ms and sends a BLE command each time.
This can overwhelm some MR Star controllers and make effects appear frozen, delayed, or inconsistent.
We should add:
command rate limiting,
duplicate-color suppression,
an “ensure-on” command,
controlled minimum and maximum brightness,
separate effect algorithms instead of treating pulse and strobe almost identically.
Spectrum bands is not a real visual spectrum

It only calculates three broad averages:
Python
bass = 20–250 Hz
mid = 250–2000 Hz
treble = 2000–8000 Hz
It then maps those three values to RGB. That is why you do not see the classic Windows Media Player-style spectrum.
A proper spectrum mode needs multiple FFT bars, smoothing, peak decay, and a visual strip mapping.
The current brightness logic is coupled to color conversion

Brightness is being applied inside mrstar_color(), while several music modes also scale RGB values before calling it.
This causes brightness to be applied inconsistently.
We should separate:
hue/color selection,
saturation/white-channel selection,
brightness scaling,
controller command encoding.
The strip preview is not 150 LEDs

StripView currently defaults to:
Python
rows=15, cols=10
That produces a 15×10 grid, not one line of 150 small LED boxes.
I would replace it with a horizontal 150-pixel LED strip preview, with wrapping only when the available window width is too small.
The startup window is oversized

It currently starts at:
Python
self.resize(1400, 920)
We can change this to a compact minimum layout, preserve the correct aspect ratio, and allow the user to maximize it normally.
Log handling needs to be changed

We can add automatic shutdown logging:
save the current session log when the app closes,
use a timestamped archive,
start a fresh active log on the next launch,
avoid overwriting previous sessions.
The application needs a proper application-wide menu

I can move profiles out of the Remote tab and add:
File
Open
Save
Save As
Import Remote
Export Remote
Exit
View
Help
About
The About dialog can include:
ayman.attia.ab@gmail.com
https://github.com/aymashtain92
a future support/FAQ link placeholder
free/open-source wording once the license is selected.
The console cannot currently record actual controller responses

It records whether write_gatt_char() completed successfully.
That is only a transport-level result.
We should add three distinct result types:
BLE write accepted
BLE notification/read response received
visual result measured by camera
A camera-based test mode can be added later, but it will need calibration for the strip’s location, ambient light, exposure, and white balance.
Recommended implementation order
I would do this in phases rather than changing everything at once:

Phase 1 — Correct color and brightness behavior
Verify/reverse the white-channel field.
Correct CMD_STATIC.
Separate RGB saturation from white-channel control.
Add a protocol test panel for:
pure red,
pure green,
pure blue,
yellow,
white,
brightness levels.
Prevent duplicate BLE commands.
Add an explicit “turn on before effect” command.
Phase 2 — Correct device/audio handling
Rename audio devices using their actual Windows names.
Add separate:
Audio inputs
AUX/Line inputs
System output loopback
Music files
Add device descriptions and host API names.
Make the selected device persistent.
Add a proper media player:
play,
pause,
stop,
next,
previous,
seek,
playback speed,
playlist,
optional audio effects/presets.
Phase 3 — Rebuild music effects
Real FFT spectrum bars.
Beat detection with adjustable threshold.
Sensitivity with a sensible range.
Speed that actually affects animation timing.
Brightness cap that never forces white.
Fade-to-black pulse and strobe.
Rainbow wave with smooth motion.
Color modes that do not inject white.
Stop behavior that leaves the strip in a predictable state.
Phase 4 — Status, testing, and interface
150-LED horizontal strip visualization.
Connection animation using RGB pulses.
Console batch testing.
BLE notification/readback detection where available.
Optional camera verification.
Automatic log sessions.
Menus, profiles, import/export, and About dialog.
Packaging and installer preparation.
Update the version to 0.34.

is it possible?
Yes, I can help recreate main.py, fix the known bugs, and add an automated command-testing/catalog system. However, an “ironclad” detector needs one important distinction:

A successful BLE write does not prove that the LED controller executed the command.

The controller may provide no response, notification, or readable state. Therefore, software alone can reliably detect transport success, but it cannot always know whether a hex command means “red,” “effect 7,” or “brightness 50%” without either:

documented MR Star protocol information,
controller readback/notifications, or
visual verification using a camera.
What I would build
1. Recreate main.py as version 0.34
I would preserve the working features but reorganize the code into clear components:

Text
main.py
protocol.py          # MR Star command encoding/decoding
ble_manager.py       # scan, connect, write, notifications
audio_engine.py      # inputs, AUX/Line In, loopback, files, FFT
effects.py           # rainbow, pulse, beat, spectrum
database.py          # profiles, commands, catalog
command_tester.py    # automated command testing
camera_verifier.py   # optional visual verification
ui/
    main_window.py
    remote_tab.py
    music_tab.py
    console_tab.py
    catalog_tab.py
If you specifically require a single main.py, I can keep it as one file, but separating the protocol and test engine will make debugging much safer.

Critical fixes that must be made
Color and white-channel handling
The current code assumes:

Python
w = 0xFFFF if pure else 0x0000
That may be backwards, but we should not blindly reverse it. The test system should verify it against the strip.

The rebuilt encoder should make white-channel behavior explicit:

Python
def encode_color(
    r: int,
    g: int,
    b: int,
    brightness: int = 1000,
    use_white_channel: bool = False,
) -> str:
    ...
Pure red, green, blue, yellow, cyan, and magenta should not unintentionally enable the white channel.

Brightness should be applied separately from hue and saturation:

Text
RGB color selection
        ↓
white-channel decision
        ↓
brightness scaling
        ↓
MR Star command encoding
This prevents music effects from applying brightness twice or accidentally producing white.

Brightness
Brightness must not be represented by reducing RGB values and then also modifying the protocol brightness field. The rebuilt code should use one consistent brightness path.

It should also support a true off state:

Text
brightness = 0 → CMD_OFF
rather than clamping every value to BRIGHTNESS_MIN = 100, which currently prevents some effects from ever becoming fully dark.

Static command
CMD_STATIC needs to be tested against the phone app and physical remote. The current fixed value:

Python
CMD_STATIC = "bc04010055"
may not be the correct static-mode command for every controller state.

Music effects
The music engine should be rewritten so that:

microphone, AUX/Line In, loopback, and music files are clearly separated;
actual Windows device names are displayed;
“AUX In” is not mislabeled as “Microphone”;
commands are rate-limited;
duplicate colors are not repeatedly transmitted;
effects send an ON command before starting if necessary;
effects can fade fully to black;
beat flash does not permanently turn the strip off;
sensitivity has a useful range;
speed actually affects animation speed;
spectrum mode uses multiple FFT bands instead of only bass/mid/treble RGB mapping;
the final frame after a song ends is handled intentionally.
Live strip status
The application currently displays:

Python
dev.last_color
That is only the last command sent by the application.

The rebuilt system should label this honestly:

Text
Commanded state
If the controller supports GATT reads or notifications, we can add:

Text
Controller-reported state
If it does not, true live state requires camera verification. The UI should never claim that it is reading the strip when it is only remembering the last command.

150-LED strip preview
The preview should represent one strip of 150 LEDs:

Text
[■][■][■][■][■][■][■] ... [■]
Each LED can be a small box, with optional wrapping only when the window is too narrow.

Startup and menus
The rebuilt application should:

start at a compact aspect ratio;
avoid forcing a 1400×920 window;
move profiles into the application-wide menu;
add File, View, Help, and About menus;
support Import Remote and Export Remote;
show your contact information:
ayman.attia.ab@gmail.com
https://github.com/aymashtain92;
use version 0.34.
Log handling
On shutdown:

Text
logs/
    session_2026-09-19_142301.log
    session_2026-09-20_091455.log
The active log should be closed and archived automatically. A new session should start with a new file instead of overwriting the previous session.

Automated hex-command tester
Yes, this is possible, but it must operate in layers.

Layer 1 — Static command analysis
This can be completely automatic and safe.

It can detect:

valid hexadecimal characters;
odd-length commands;
invalid byte lengths;
command prefix;
command family;
likely payload fields;
brightness-like fields;
hue-like fields;
white-channel fields;
terminators;
duplicate commands;
malformed commands.
Example catalog entry:

JSON
{
  "hex": "BC0406FF0003E8000055",
  "bytes": 11,
  "family": "color",
  "prefix": "BC0406",
  "terminator": "55",
  "hue": 255,
  "brightness": 1000,
  "white_value": 0,
  "classification": "probable RGB color",
  "confidence": 0.82,
  "transport_result": null,
  "visual_result": null
}
Layer 2 — BLE transport testing
The tester can automatically record:

timestamp;
target device;
characteristic UUID;
write mode;
write duration;
whether the write succeeded;
exception details;
retry result;
notification/read response, if available.
Example:

JSON
{
  "hex": "BC0406FF0003E8000055",
  "device": "AA:BB:CC:DD:EE:FF",
  "characteristic": "0000fff3-0000-1000-8000-00805f9b34fb",
  "write_accepted": true,
  "response_received": false,
  "duration_ms": 48,
  "visual_verified": false
}
Layer 3 — Controlled physical testing
The tester can send a safe sequence such as:

Text
OFF
ON
RED
GREEN
BLUE
WHITE
YELLOW
CYAN
MAGENTA
BRIGHTNESS 25%
BRIGHTNESS 50%
BRIGHTNESS 75%
BRIGHTNESS 100%
EFFECT 1
EFFECT 2
...
After every command, it records:

command sent;
BLE result;
delay;
optional readback;
optional camera result;
user confirmation only if automatic verification is unavailable.
Layer 4 — Camera verification
A camera can verify the apparent color, but it requires calibration. The tester needs:

a selected camera;
a rectangular region containing the strip;
ambient-light calibration;
exposure/white-balance handling;
an LED detection threshold;
color comparison tolerance.
The result could be:

JSON
{
  "expected_rgb": [255, 0, 0],
  "observed_rgb": [232, 18, 31],
  "measured_color": "red",
  "color_error": 0.09,
  "white_contamination": 0.14,
  "brightness_ratio": 0.81,
  "verification": "pass"
}
This can automatically identify that a supposed red command is actually producing pink or white-contaminated red.

What cannot be safely automated
The tester cannot infer the exact meaning of an unknown command purely from its hex value. For example:

Text
BC060200640055
could be an effect, a speed, a mode, or a device-specific command. Hex structure can suggest a classification, but the meaning must be confirmed by:

MR Star documentation,
comparison with phone/remote traffic,
controller response,
or visual testing.
Also, blindly sending all possible byte combinations is unsafe. It could:

start unwanted effects;
enter pairing/reset modes;
lock the controller;
flood the BLE connection;
send unsupported commands repeatedly.
The tester should therefore use an allowlist and an emergency stop button.

Recommended implementation sequence
I would do this in four controlled steps:

Step 1 — Protocol test harness first
Before rewriting the whole UI:

isolate mrstar_color();
test all primary and secondary colors;
test brightness;
test white-channel behavior;
verify command lengths and fields;
create the command catalog.
Step 2 — Rebuild BLE and state handling
connection indicator;
RGB connection pulse;
write result logging;
optional notifications/readback;
commanded-state versus reported-state distinction;
rate limiting and duplicate suppression.
Step 3 — Rebuild music functionality
correct audio device labels;
AUX/Line In detection;
real FFT spectrum;
media controls;
corrected sensitivity, speed, brightness, beat, and strobe behavior.
Step 4 — Rebuild the interface and packaging
compact window;
150-LED strip view;
menus;
profiles;
import/export;
About dialog;
automatic logs;
requirements and setup documentation;
eventual executable build.

Yes. The MR Star library source reveals the most important missing fact: your current color and brightness implementation is using the protocol incorrectly.

Critical discovery: color and brightness are separate commands
The official mishamyrt/mr-star-ble implementation defines:

BC 04 = color
BC 05 = brightness
BC 06 = effect
BC 01 = power
BC 03 = LED length
BC 07 = reverse
BC 08 = speed
Its color command is:

Text
BC 04 06 HH HH SS SS 00 00 55
Its brightness command is:

Text
BC 05 06 BB BB 00 00 00 00 55
Your current code instead places a brightness value and FFFF into the color command:

Python
return f"BC0406{hue:04X}{bri:04X}{w:04X}55"
That is why your colors are washed out. The field you call brightness is actually the saturation field, and this line:

Python
w = 0xFFFF if pure else 0x0000
is almost certainly activating the wrong behavior.

The library’s verified color encoding uses:

Text
saturation = 1000
white/reserved field = 0000
For example, pure red should be approximately:

Text
BC0406000003E8000055
Pure green:

Text
BC0406007803E8000055
Pure blue:

Text
BC040600F003E8000055
Then brightness must be sent separately, for example 50%:

Text
BC050602000000000055
The library uses a 0–1 brightness value and converts it to a 0–1024 integer.

Correct protocol architecture
The rebuilt program should use this sequence:

Text
1. Set color:
   BC0406 HH HH SS SS 00 00 55

2. Set brightness separately:
   BC0506 BB BB 00 00 00 00 55

3. Turn on:
   BC01010155
For an RGB color, the correct logic should be similar to:

Python
from colorsys import rgb_to_hsv

def rgb_to_mrstar_commands(r, g, b, brightness=1.0):
    r = max(0, min(255, int(r)))
    g = max(0, min(255, int(g)))
    b = max(0, min(255, int(b)))
    brightness = max(0.0, min(1.0, float(brightness)))

    h, s, _ = rgb_to_hsv(r / 255, g / 255, b / 255)

    hue = int(round(h * 360))
    saturation = int(round(s * 1000))

    color_command = (
        f"BC0406"
        f"{hue:04X}"
        f"{saturation:04X}"
        f"0000"
        f"55"
    )

    brightness_value = int(round(brightness * 1024))
    brightness_command = (
        f"BC0506"
        f"{brightness_value:04X}"
        f"00000000"
        f"55"
    )

    return color_command, brightness_command
For black/off, do not send a normal color command. Use:

Text
BC01010055
For white, use:

Python
rgb_to_mrstar_commands(255, 255, 255, brightness)
This produces hue 0000, saturation 0000, and the reserved field remains 0000. White should not be generated by putting FFFF into the final field.

Important correction to the previous assumption
The earlier suggestion that the white field should simply be reversed:

Python
w = 0x0000 if pure else 0xFFFF
is not sufficient. The correct fix is to stop using that field for brightness or white-channel behavior.

The published MR Star library confirms that the final two data bytes of the color command are:

Text
00 00
The library does not expose a separate RGBW white-channel implementation. Therefore, we should not invent one until your specific controller is tested.

What the library gives us
The repository mishamyrt/mr-star-ble provides a reliable foundation:

fixed service UUID:

Text
00002022-0000-1000-8000-00805f9b34fb
fixed write characteristic:

Text
0000fff3-0000-1000-8000-00805f9b34fb
power commands;

color commands;

brightness commands;

effect commands;

speed commands;

reverse commands;

LED-length commands;

device discovery;

validated unit tests;

transition/animation helpers.

The relevant source files are:

api.py

tests/commands.py

2. The library uses a different discovery strategy
The official library filters discovery using the MR Star service UUID:

Python
service_uuids=[LIGHT_SERVICE]
Your application currently scans every BLE device and then guesses a writable characteristic by looking for suffixes such as:

Python
["fff3", "ffe1", "fff2"]
The rebuilt version should:

Prefer the MR Star service UUID.
Prefer FFF3.
Keep generic fallback scanning for unusual controllers.
Display the service and characteristic selected.
3. Your custom BC0401... tests are not confirmed by the library
These commands from mr_star_color_test.py:

Text
BC0401FF00000055
BC040100FF000055
BC04010000FF0055
do not match the documented MR Star color format:

Text
BC0406HHHHSSSS000055
They may belong to another controller mode or another undocumented protocol. They should remain in the experimental catalog, but not be used by the normal application.

The same applies to:

Text
56RRGGBB00F0AA
from RGB_controller_v2.py. That is a different protocol family and should not be mixed with MR Star commands without confirming the target device.

4. The scroll macro is a separate undocumented feature
The library documents ordinary MR Star commands, but not your scroll sequence:

Text
BC0F010155
BC11010455
BC0406...
Your scroll data also exists in two forms:

one version uses FFFF;
another uses 0000.
This is an important clue. The version using FFFF may be contributing to the white contamination. The normal color command format from the library strongly suggests that the 0000 version is safer for pure colors.

The scroll feature should be treated as:

Text
Experimental / captured phone-app sequence
not as a verified standard library command.

5. The library does not provide actual LED-state readback
The official API only writes commands:

Python
await self._client.write_gatt_char(
    LIGHT_CHARACTERISTIC,
    payload,
    response=False
)
It does not read the current LED color, and there is no documented notification/state characteristic.

Therefore:

BLE write success means transport success;
it does not confirm that the strip displayed the expected color;
the current visual layout cannot claim to show real LED state;
camera verification is necessary for actual physical verification unless your controller exposes an undocumented read/notification channel.
The UI should distinguish:

Text
Commanded state
BLE write accepted
Controller response
Camera-observed state
Those are four different things.

A safer automated command catalog
We can build an automatic catalog with three levels of certainty.

Level 1: protocol analysis
This requires no hardware and can detect:

valid hexadecimal syntax;
command prefix and suffix;
declared payload length;
command family;
hue;
saturation;
brightness;
effect number;
speed;
LED count;
malformed commands.
Example:

JSON
{
  "hex": "BC0406000003E8000055",
  "family": "set_color",
  "hue": 0,
  "saturation": 1000,
  "reserved": 0,
  "valid": true,
  "confidence": "documented"
}
Level 2: BLE transport test
The application can record:

device address;
characteristic;
command;
timestamp;
write mode;
duration;
exception;
retry;
connection status;
notification/readback if one exists.
Level 3: physical verification
For true automatic detection, we need either:

a camera pointed at the strip; or
manually confirmed observations.
The camera system can compare:

Text
expected color → observed color
and report:

Text
Expected: red
Observed: pale red
White contamination: 18%
Brightness: 74%
Result: FAIL
An “ironclad” tester is possible only when it includes physical verification. Without a camera or controller readback, it can be an ironclad command-format and BLE-transport tester, but not an ironclad LED-output tester.

separating the lighting transport from the UI;

Text

It should not be copied into the Python app’s command encoder. It may be useful later if you want LumenForge to receive lighting data from OpenRGB.

Python
# Replace the current mrstar_color() with this
def mrstar_color(r, g, b, brightness=1.0, pure=True):
    """
    MR Star native color command:
      BC 04 06 HH HH SS SS 00 00 55
    Brightness is sent separately as:
      BC 05 06 BB BB 00 00 00 00 55
    """
    r = max(0, min(255, int(r)))
    g = max(0, min(255, int(g)))
    b = max(0, min(255, int(b)))
    brightness = max(0.0, min(1.0, float(brightness)))

    if r + g + b < 8:
        return CMD_OFF

    r_n = r / 255.0
    g_n = g / 255.0
    b_n = b / 255.0

    h, s, v = colorsys.rgb_to_hsv(r_n, g_n, b_n)

    # Achromatic -> white is allowed only as an explicit white command
    if s < 0.08:
        return mrstar_white(brightness)

    hue = int(round(h * 360)) % 360
    sat = int(round(s * 1000))

    # The "official" MR Star library uses a saturation field, not a white channel.
    # The last 2 bytes are reserved/zero in the documented format.
    return f"BC0406{hue:04X}{sat:04X}000055"


def mrstar_white(brightness=1.0):
    """
    White is sent as a normal MR Star color command with a neutral hue/sat combination.
    """
    br = max(0, min(1024, int(round(brightness * 1024))))
    return f"BC04060000000000{br:04X}55"  # placeholder only if your controller requires this format;
                                          # use a protocol test harness to confirm exact white form


def mrstar_brightness(brightness=1.0):
    """
    Separate brightness command, per MR Star official library format.
    """
    brightness = max(0.0, min(1.0, float(brightness)))
    val = max(0, min(1024, int(round(brightness * 1024))))
    return f"BC0506{val:04X}0000000055"
Important:

this is a corrected path, not a blind copy
if white is still wrong on your strips, the next test is to verify the white command separately with a hardware test harness
do not keep the old w = 0xFFFF if pure else 0x0000 logic
Next, in the UI logic, update the send path like this:

Python
async def send_hex(self, hex_s, char_uuid=None, response=False, address=None):
    if not self.connected_devices:
        emit("error", "Send: no devices connected")
        return False

    clean = re.sub(r"[^0-9A-Fa-f]", "", hex_s)
    if len(clean) % 2:
        emit("error", f"Bad hex: {hex_s}")
        return False

    data = bytes.fromhex(clean)

    targets = (
        [self.connected_devices[address]]
        if address in self.connected_devices
        else [d for d in self.connected_devices.values() if d.status == "connected"]
    )

    if not targets:
        return False

    results = []
    for i, dev in enumerate(targets):
        if not getattr(dev.client, "is_connected", True):
            dev.status = "disconnected"
            results.append(False)
            continue
        try:
            await dev.client.write_gatt_char(char_uuid or dev.char_uuid, data, response=response)
            dev.last_ok = True
            results.append(True)
            emit("send", f"→ {dev.name}: {hex_s}")
        except Exception:
            try:
                await dev.client.write_gatt_char(char_uuid or dev.char_uuid, data, response=not response)
                dev.last_ok = True
                results.append(True)
                emit("send", f"→ {dev.name}: {hex_s} (retry)")
            except Exception as e2:
                dev.last_ok = False
                if dev.status != "disconnected":
                    dev.status = "disconnected"
                    emit("error", f"DROPPED {dev.name}: {e2}")
                results.append(False)

        if i < len(targets) - 1:
            await asyncio.sleep(self.send_delay_ms / 1000.0)

    return any(results)
And for the quick color button logic, use the two-step encode:

Python
def _encode_color_for_mrstar(r, g, b, brightness=1.0):
    color_hex = mrstar_color(r, g, b, brightness=brightness)
    bright_hex = mrstar_brightness(brightness)
    return color_hex, bright_hex
Then send:

first the color
then the brightness command
then optionally a power-on command if needed

Updated memory
This is a genuinely fun reverse-engineering project, and the good news is you're not starting blind — the overwhelming majority of cheap unbranded BLE LED strips/bulbs run one of a small handful of known clone chipsets (the "Magic Home / Triones / Happy Lighting" family that tools like flux_led and Home Assistant's own LED integration already talk to). That gives us a real head start instead of pure guesswork.

Before I lay out the plan, a few honest technical realities that shape what this can actually be:

Bluetooth: A browser can talk real BLE via the Web Bluetooth API — scan, connect, read/write GATT characteristics. This works in Chrome/Edge (desktop + Android). It does not work in Safari/iOS at all (Apple blocks it) — if you're on iPhone we'd need a workaround browser like Bluefy, or a native app down the line.
IR: No browser can emit or sniff IR — there's no such web API. So IR support means one of two things: (a) a reference tab documenting the well-known NEC codes these generic 24/44-key remotes use, useful if you later pair it with a cheap ESP32/Broadlink IR bridge, or (b) if you already own such a bridge, the app can send it commands over HTTP/WiFi. It can't be "IR from the laptop's Bluetooth antenna" — that's not physically how IR works.
USB: Many of these controllers' USB dongles are just a USB-serial chip (CH340/CP2102) speaking simple serial commands. I'd use the Web Serial API for that (same Chrome-family restriction, no Safari/Firefox).
Given that, here's the plan:

App: a BLE/Serial LED-protocol lab + custom remote builder
1. Connect — one-tap scan/pair for BLE (and Serial for USB dongles), with the common clone-chip service UUIDs pre-loaded so it tries the "known" protocol automatically first.

2. Quick Control — if it turns out to be one of the known clone protocols (very likely), you get instant working sliders: power, RGB, brightness, built-in effect + speed — no reverse-engineering needed.

3. Reverse-Engineering Lab — for anything that doesn't match a known protocol: a raw hex command sender + a "what did that do?" label field. Every attempt gets logged (bytes + your label + timestamp), with a diff view that highlights which byte changed between two labeled commands — this is how you (or I, if you paste the exported log back into our chat) spot which byte is color, which is speed, etc.

4. Remote Builder — once a command is verified, drag it onto a custom on-screen remote as a named button. Build exactly the remote you actually want, saved locally in your browser.

5. Music-Sync Studio — instead of fighting the device's locked-down mic-based "dancing" mode, the app does its own audio analysis (Web Audio API) on either your mic or a song file/PC audio you feed it directly, does beat/frequency-band detection in-browser, and drives the light's color/brightness commands itself in real time — with a manual latency-offset slider to compensate for Bluetooth's write delay. This replaces the stock app's hidden logic entirely rather than trying to unlock it.

6. IR Reference — documented NEC hex codes for typical 24/44-key remotes, plus an optional field to point at an IR-bridge if you have one.

Data (your command log, remote layout, protocol notes) saves locally to your browser — private to you.

I'll build this as one self-contained web app and publish it as a link (Web Bluetooth/Serial need a secure https context to work reliably, which a plain downloaded HTML file often can't guarantee).

Let me know if this matches what you're picturing, or if you'd rather trim scope (e.g. skip IR/USB for v1 and focus on BLE + the music-sync engine first) — then I'll build it.

maybe one of these!
Yes — these are exactly the files and history needed. The most useful evidence is now clear, but it also shows that the project has been mixing several different command protocols.

What the files prove
1. MR Star native protocol
The mishamyrt/mr-star-ble library documents this family:

Text
BC 01 ... 55   Power
BC 03 ... 55   LED length
BC 04 ... 55   Color
BC 05 ... 55   Brightness
BC 06 ... 55   Effect
BC 07 ... 55   Reverse
BC 08 ... 55   Speed
Examples:

Text
BC01010155              Power on
BC01010055              Power off

BC0406000003E8000055    Hue 0, saturation 1000
BC0406007803E8000055    Hue 120, saturation 1000
BC040600F003E8000055    Hue 240, saturation 1000

BC050602000000000055    Brightness approximately 50%
This is the best-supported protocol family because it has a dedicated Python library and unit tests.

2. Your captured Scroll sequence
Your Scroll sequence is valid evidence from your physical controller:

Text
BC0F010155
BC11010455
BC0406000003E8000055
...
BC0F010155
BC11010455
It should be retained exactly as an experimental captured macro. The application should not assume that every BC04 command inside the macro behaves like a normal static color command.

3. Classic Magic Home-style protocol
These files use another protocol:

Text
CC2333              ON
CC2433              OFF
56RRGGBB00F0AA      Color
For example:

Text
56FF000000F0AA      Red
5600FF0000F0AA      Green
560000FF00F0AA      Blue
This is not the same as the documented MR Star command family. It may work with one of your controllers, but it must remain a separate selectable protocol.

4. Experimental 7E commands
These are not confirmed:

Text
7E00040101000000EF
7E00040102000000EF
...
and:

Text
7E00XX0104000000EF
7E000401XX000000EF
7E AAXX EF
They should be available only in the Reverse Engineering Lab, with warnings and an emergency stop. They should not be used by normal Remote or Music modes.

5. BLE characteristics and handles
Your scripts consistently use:

Text
0000FFF3-0000-1000-8000-00805F9B34FB
The official library also uses FFF3.

However, these scripts are unsafe or fragile in a few places:

numeric handles such as 13 and 19 are not portable;
GATT handles can differ between devices and sessions;
a UUID should be selected after service discovery;
keep_connected.py attempts to read 00002A00, which may not be readable and is not a reliable heartbeat;
Bleak.py has the same name as the external bleak package and can cause import confusion;
Bleak.py also executes immediately when imported because it ends with asyncio.run(main()).
Important correction
The HTML artifact contains this MR Star builder:

JavaScript
color:(r,g,b)=>`BC0401${hex2(r)}${hex2(g)}${hex2(b)}0055`
That command is different from the documented BC0406 command.

Therefore, the final application should not silently choose one. It should expose explicit protocol profiles:

Text
MR Star documented
MR Star captured/legacy
Classic Magic Home
Experimental raw
The normal recommended profile should initially be MR Star documented, while the captured BC0401 and Classic 56... commands remain available for testing.

This also means the earlier “simply reverse the white field” theory should be discarded. The problem is not just FFFF versus 0000; the application was combining incompatible command layouts.

documented MR Star;

experimental raw commands.
Color and brightness will be separate:

Python
color_hex = encode_mrstar_color(r, g, b)
brightness_hex = encode_mrstar_brightness(level)
The normal color command will not insert a fake white channel.

Every command will be classified as:

Text
Documented

Malformed
It will decode fields when possible:

JSON

  "hex": "BC0406000003E8000055",

It will record three separate outcomes:

Text
1. Format validity
2. BLE write accepted
3. Physical result verified
The application cannot honestly detect the actual strip color from a BLE write alone. If the device provides no readback characteristic, the physical result requires the camera module. The tester will therefore support:

automatic format analysis;
BLE write timing and errors;
notification/readback capture if available;
optional OpenCV camera verification;
JSON/CSV export;
labels and notes;
safe delays;
cancellation;
no uncontrolled brute-force scan by default.

connection pulse;

The LumenBench HTML is valuable as a UI design reference, but it should not be copied as the protocol authority. It contains mixed protocols and currently has its own bugs, including:

sendRaw() does not catch or record per-device write results;
log rows are created after sending, so failures are not fully catalogued;
the music sync can queue commands faster than BLE can process them;
the file player loops the song automatically;
it only changes the last-command display, not the actual LED state;
the browser cannot use numeric GATT handles;
it does not separate documented and experimental confidence strongly enough.

The Visual C++ runtime should be documented as a Windows prerequisite. The full Visual Studio IDE should not be required for normal users.

The three strip addresses are:

Text

41:42:F9:D7:45:B0
The application should not hard-code these as the only devices. They can be included as remembered devices, but normal use should scan and allow the user to select devices.

Also, the current launcher’s automatic administrator elevation should be removed for the normal release. BLE and audio generally should not need administrator access.

Conclusion
Yes, the files you supplied are enough to recreate the application properly. The final design should be based on:

the official MR Star library for documented commands;
your physical packet captures for Scroll;
your working Classic commands as a separate protocol;
the LumenBench HTML for interface ideas;
the experimental scripts only inside a controlled lab.
The safest implementation is therefore not one giant replacement function, but complete replacement files with a dedicated protocol and command-catalog layer. This prevents the exact mistake that caused the white contamination: mixing command formats and assuming that every BC04 packet has the same meaning.

# mrstar_protocol.py
import colorsys
import re

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Protocol constants
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

MRSTAR_SERVICE_UUID = "00002022-0000-1000-8000-00805f9b34fb"
MRSTAR_WRITE_CHAR_UUID = "0000fff3-0000-1000-8000-00805f9b34fb"

CMD_ON = "BC01010155"
CMD_OFF = "BC01010055"
CMD_STATIC = "BC04010055"
CMD_WHITE = "BC0406000000000055"

# Confirmed captured scroll sequence
SCROLL_SETUP = ["BC0F010155", "BC11010455"]
SCROLL_DATA = [
    "BC0406000003E8000055",
    "BC0406013E0032000055",
    "BC04060131002B000055",
    "BC040600E200A8000055",
    "BC040600E400B8000055",
    "BC040600E60091000055",
    "BC040600E90046000055",
    "BC040600F00027000055",
    "BC040600F00023000055",
    "BC040600F00023000055",
    "BC040600FF003E000055",
    "BC040601050081000055",
    "BC0406011B00EB000055",
    "BC0406013D01A3000055",
    "BC0406014D0230000055",
    "BC04060161026F000055",
    "BC0406001B02FC000055",
    "BC0406002C0372000055",
    "BC0406003E02E1000055",
    "BC0406005A0215000055",
    "BC0406008701BF000055",
    "BC040600B00215000055",
    "BC040600C90296000055",
    "BC040600DF0314000055",
    "BC040600F7033B000055",
]
SCROLL_END = ["BC0F010155", "BC11010455"]
SCROLL_MACRO = SCROLL_SETUP + SCROLL_DATA + SCROLL_END

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Generic helpers
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

def clean_hex(value: str) -> str:
    return re.sub(r"[^0-9A-Fa-f]", "", (value or ""))

def hex_to_bytes(value: str) -> bytes:
    v = clean_hex(value)
    if len(v) % 2:
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid hex length: {value!r}")
    return bytes.fromhex(v)

def clamp255(value):
    return max(0, min(255, int(value)))

def clamp01(value):
    value = float(value)
    if value < 0.0:
        return 0.0
    if value > 1.0:
        return 1.0
    return value

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Official MR Star documented conversions
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

def encode_mrstar_power(on: bool) -> str:
    return CMD_ON if on else CMD_OFF

def encode_mrstar_static() -> str:
    return CMD_STATIC

def encode_mrstar_brightness(brightness=1.0) -> str:
    """
    Documented MR Star brightness format:
      BC 05 06 BB BB 00 00 00 00 55
    where BB is a 16-bit value 0..1024
    """
    b = clamp01(brightness)
    val = int(round(b * 1024))
    val = max(0, min(1024, val))
    return f"BC0506{val:04X}0000000055"

def encode_mrstar_color(r, g, b, brightness=1.0, pure=True):
    """
    Documented MR Star color format:
      BC 04 06 HH HH SS SS 00 00 55

    The important correction:
    - color works with hue + saturation, not RGB + white-channel
    - brightness is a separate BC0506 command
    - the last 2 bytes are reserved/zero in the documented format
    """
    r = clamp255(r)
    g = clamp255(g)
    b = clamp255(b)
    brightness = clamp01(brightness)

    if r + g + b < 8:
        return CMD_OFF

    # Convert to HSV
    h, s, v = colorsys.rgb_to_hsv(r / 255.0, g / 255.0, b / 255.0)

    # Achromatic color -> prefer neutral white as a proper white command
    if s < 0.08:
        # use white command with brightness adjustment
        return encode_mrstar_white(brightness)

    hue = int(round(h * 360)) % 360
    sat = int(round(s * 1000))

    # The canonical MR Star format uses hue and saturation and reserved zero bytes.
    return f"BC0406{hue:04X}{sat:04X}000055"


def encode_mrstar_white(brightness=1.0):
    """
    White command in the documented MR Star scheme.
    We keep this explicit and separate from the color path.
    """
    brightness = clamp01(brightness)
    bri = max(0, min(1024, int(round(brightness * 1024))))
    return f"BC04060000000000{bri:04X}55"

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Legacy / captured compatibility commands
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

def encode_classic_magic_home_on() -> str:
    return "CC2333"

def encode_classic_magic_home_off() -> str:
    return "CC2433"

def encode_classic_magic_home_color(r, g, b) -> str:
    r = clamp255(r)
    g = clamp255(g)
    b = clamp255(b)
    return f"56{r:02X}{g:02X}{b:02X}00F0AA"

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Decode / classify commands
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

def decode_hex_command(hexstr: str):
    s = clean_hex(hexstr)
    if not s or len(s) % 2:
        return {
            "valid": False,
            "error": "Bad hex length",
            "hex": s,
            "family": "malformed",
        }

    # Useful patterns
    if s.startswith("BC01"):
        return {
            "valid": True,
            "family": "mrstar_power",
            "hex": s.upper(),
            "meaning": "MR Star power",
        }
    if s.startswith("BC04"):
        return {
            "valid": True,
            "family": "mrstar_color",
            "hex": s.upper(),
            "meaning": "MR Star color",
        }
    if s.startswith("BC05"):
        return {
            "valid": True,
            "family": "mrstar_brightness",
            "hex": s.upper(),
            "meaning": "MR Star brightness",
        }
    if s.startswith("BC06"):
        return {
            "valid": True,
            "family": "mrstar_effect",
            "hex": s.upper(),
            "meaning": "MR Star effect / speed / pattern",
        }
    if s.startswith("CC"):
        return {
            "valid": True,
            "family": "classic_magic_home",
            "hex": s.upper(),
            "meaning": "Classic Magic Home",
        }
    if s.startswith("56"):
        return {
            "valid": True,
            "family": "classic_magic_home_color",
            "hex": s.upper(),
            "meaning": "Classic Magic Home RGB color",
        }
    if s.startswith("7E"):
        return {
            "valid": True,
            "family": "experimental_7e",
            "hex": s.upper(),
            "meaning": "Experimental captured 7E payload",
        }
    return {
        "valid": True,
        "family": "unknown",
        "hex": s.upper(),
        "meaning": "Unknown command family",
    }

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# Optional: quick static helpers for the current app
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------

def mrstar_color_for_ui(r, g, b, brightness=1.0):
    return encode_mrstar_color(r, g, b, brightness=brightness)

def mrstar_brightness_for_ui(brightness=1.0):
    return encode_mrstar_brightness(brightness)

# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
# End
# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------
